# Metal conformance coverage

Generated by `scripts/conformance-coverage/coverage.py` from the suite's own sources and its committed recordings. Do not edit this file; change the sources or the suite and run the script.

This is not [METAL-COVERAGE.md](METAL-COVERAGE.md), which says what we **implement**. This says what the conformance suite **compares against Metal**. An item counts as *exercised* here only where a case's native Metal leg calls it; a case's Metal answer is a recorded fact only where the recording is committed. The two claims are different sizes, and only the second is conformance.

It says it along **two axes**, because either one alone reads as more than it is. The **items** axis is reach: one call of a selector is enough, so a selector that takes an enum counts as exercised whatever value reached it. The **option space** axis is depth: of each Metal enum's and option set's values, which ones the suite compared. A high number on the first and a low one on the second is what a suite that touches everything shallowly looks like.

Both are **per value**, which is the limit the suite's own blind spot sits in: a side can be right about every value of two types alone and wrong about a cell of the two together. *By crossing* inside the option-space axis counts what a case puts to Metal at once, from declarations the generator checks: the pairs of the option space, and — counted apart and never added in — the crossings that are no pair of it. They are the only numbers here about interactions, and each is a floor by every crossing nobody declared.

## What this measures, and what it cannot

The suite reaches Metal through `objc2-metal`, whose Rust name for a binding is the selector with each `:` written `_`. So a call site names its selector outright, and the derivation resolves the **receiver's type** by following what made it — a class's constructor, a getter whose answer `objc2_metal.tsv` records, a field of the side's own struct, a parameter's declared type — and then walks that type's supertypes to the container the SDK declares the item on. A message to a class, written `MTLType::selector(..)`, names its own receiver and needs no typing.

Its limits, measured rather than asserted:

- **1502 of 1673 call sites** in the Metal leg resolve to an item of the SDK. 53 of those resolve because only one type in `objc2-metal` binds the name at all, so no typing was needed.
- **2 call sites send a selector `objc2-metal` binds on no Metal type at all**, so they are an item of no Metal container and there is no count for them to cost: `alloc`, `class`, which are `NSObject`'s.
- **130 call sites resolve to no item although some Metal type binds the selector**: an instance send whose receiver this could not type, or a message to a class that does not bind the name sent to it. Each sent its selector to exactly one receiver, so each exercised one item; which one is what the typing could not say. **Every one of their selectors is bound by at least one item some other call site credits**, so none of these sites proves an item is missing from the count. That is not a proof that the count is complete: such a site may have sent its selector to a type other than the credited one, and this cannot tell. To rule it out, spell the receiver's type at the call site — a `let` with a type, rather than a pattern binding or an inferred one — and this re-derives with the item credited to the case that calls it.
- **0 call sites typed to a receiver that does not bind the selector sent to it.** The generator fails rather than writing this document if any does, which is what keeps the typing from quietly counting the wrong item.
- **96 of the map's implemented rows are a container's own**: the `class`, `protocol` and `category` rows, whose name is the container's and not a selector's. No call site can resolve to one, because `objc2-metal` binds no selector by that name, so these are credited by the suite **messaging** the type instead — a call whose receiver is the container or a subtype of it — under the same gate and the same family attribution as the selector it sends.
- **12 are the header's free functions**, which no container declares. `objc2-metal` binds these as Rust functions, so the receiver typing never sees one; the leg calls such a function by its own C name and the walk reads it as an edge of the call graph.
- **3 implemented items `objc2-metal` binds no name for at all**, so no case can call them whatever it is authored to do. They are counted apart from the gap below rather than folded into it, because a gap is work and these are not:
  - `MTLDevice`'s `programmableSamplePositionsSupported`
  - `MTLDevice`'s `rasterOrderGroupsSupported`
  - `MTLFunctionReflection`'s `userAnnotation`
- The unit a call is attributed to is the **case module**, not the case: a module's cases share one `run`, so which of them encodes which step is not a property of the text. Where a module holds one case the two are the same.
- **No verdict is recorded here.** A verdict is what a replay on one venue measured, and a number like that in a generated document is a journal entry that rots. What is durable, and is here, is whether a case compares **exactly**: the allowances a module declares, and the rows of the suite's ledger.

A tolerance names a **site of a transcript**, not an item, so the last two rows are read as written: a case that loosens one site of its own transcript may compare every other field of it exactly, and this derivation cannot tell which of a case's fields came from which Metal call. Where the distinction matters, read the case.

*Exercised* and *recorded* are one number here, not two, and deliberately: a case is credited with exercising an item only through its cases whose answer record mode took by **running** Metal, and such a case has a committed recording by construction. A case that carries an answer, or whose call Metal never returned from, runs no native leg and exercises nothing, however much Metal its code names.

## By option space, which is depth rather than reach

The axis above counts a Metal **item** as exercised where one call of it ran. A selector that takes an enum therefore counts whatever value reached it, so that axis says nothing about how much of an enum the suite ever put to Metal. This one does, over the values themselves.

The denominator is generated, never written here: `crates/metal2vulkan/src/metal/generated.rs` and the front end's hold every enumerator name and ordinal `scripts/regen-metal-enums/` extracts from the SDK's Metal headers, and `scripts/conformance-coverage/metal_enumerators.tsv` holds the same for the Metal types this tree declares by hand instead, which have no generated Rust. A Metal type neither covers has **no denominator here** and is listed apart, below.

A value is **compared** where either of two things holds, which are different claims:

- **answered** — the enumerator's name is in what a site of a committed recording **observed**, in an answer Metal **ran**. Metal named the value back at a site the comparison reads, so the value is in the compared text itself.
- **at a site** — the enumerator's name is a committed recording's site **name**, and in nothing that site observed: 276 of them. The comparison pairs sites by name, so the value is in the compared text and the case did put it to Metal — but the name is the **case's** label for the site, so it is no evidence that Metal said the value. It is its own column for that reason: read together with *answered* it reads as Metal having named back values that a case spelled itself, which for `MTLPixelFormat` is most of them.
- **in effect** — a case module's own text names the value, and that module has at least one case whose recording is a Metal run. Metal's recorded answer was taken with the value in effect, so what is compared is Metal's behaviour **under** it, even where no site reads it back. A blend factor is this and not the first: what the recording holds is the texels Metal blended with it.

What this axis cannot see, measured rather than asserted:

- **A value Metal answers as a bare ordinal.** A transcript holds a `uint`, and nothing in it says which enum the number belongs to, so only a site that records the enumerator's **name** is read. The *answered* column is a floor by every site that records an ordinal instead.
- **A spelling of a value this derivation does not read.** Both numerators read names, and a name written in a shape the reading does not cover is counted nowhere and looks exactly like a value no case reaches. Four shapes are read: a variant beside its type, `objc2`'s own spelling, a renaming `use`, and a braced `use` whose variants the module then writes bare. A fifth shape would be invisible the same way, so every count here is a floor by that unknown — and the two shapes already found were each caught by someone checking a count against the code, not by this derivation, so the unknown is not small on the evidence.
- **A value a case states as a raw ordinal.** A case may hand the Metal leg an enum's ordinal as a number — a sweep over `0..n` rather than over named variants — and no reading of the text can say which value a computed number is. So *in effect*, and the *stated* column of the hand-modelled table below, are floors by every sweep written that way: a type they report short of its values may in fact be swept exhaustively, and the case is what says so. Read the case before taking a row of the work list as work.
- **Which case put a value in effect.** The unit is the case module, as it is for the items axis: a value named anywhere in a module whose cases include a Metal run counts for that module, even where one of its cases names it and the rest do not.
- **A value only the suite's shared sources name** — 23 of them. Some case puts it to Metal through the render or texture vocabulary, but which case is not a property of the text, so it is neither compared nor missing and has its own column.
- **Combinations.** Every column here is per value. A type whose values are all compared says nothing about the pairs and triples of them, which is where most of the behaviour an implementation can get wrong lives. *By crossing* below counts what a case states together, in two numbers kept apart: the pairs of this option space, and the crossings that are no pair of it — an enum with a boolean or numeric field, a type with itself in two slots. Each is a floor by every crossing nobody declared.
- **Metal's API validation is excluded.** Its complaints name enumerators, and the suite records that leg and never compares it, so reading it would count Apple's prose as a comparison. So are the cost and timing tables: a site name there is what was measured, not an answer that was compared.
- **A `#[cfg(test)]` item is excluded.** One of the suite's unit tests names four blend factors to assert that no case uses them; counting those would have this report the opposite of what the code says.

|---|---|

The share is weakly informative on its own: `MTLPixelFormat`, `MTLDataType`, `MTLAttributeFormat`, `MTLVertexFormat` hold 506 of the 757 values between them, so an aggregate over types this uneven is mostly theirs. The per-type table is the one to read.

### The values no case can put to Metal

Counted apart from the gap above, because a gap is work and these are not. Each is declared in `scripts/conformance-coverage/unreachable_values.tsv` with the measurement that settled it and the case module that holds the finding, and this document is **not** written if a value listed there turns out to be one some case compared, or one its type does not have. Nothing is listed for being obscure, out of scope, or merely unbuilt: a Metal value this suite has simply not reached yet is `missing`, which is the column to read as work.

- **`MTLDataType`**, 16 of 97: no public Metal API answers one: an imageblock binding is MTLImageBlockArgument (tile) or MTLImageBlockDataArgument (fragment) and conforms to none of MTLBinding's four public subprotocols, its members sitting behind structType / imageBlockMasterStructMembers on the private MTLBindingInternal superclass, and MSL rejects a packed pixel type as a buffer pointee ("cannot be used in buffer pointee type"), so there is no second route.
  Held by `cases/tile_pipeline_reflection.rs`: `MTLDataTypeR16Snorm`, `MTLDataTypeR16Unorm`, `MTLDataTypeR8Snorm`, `MTLDataTypeR8Unorm`, `MTLDataTypeRG11B10Float`, `MTLDataTypeRG16Snorm`, `MTLDataTypeRG16Unorm`, `MTLDataTypeRG8Snorm`, `MTLDataTypeRG8Unorm`, `MTLDataTypeRGB10A2Unorm`, `MTLDataTypeRGB9E5Float`, `MTLDataTypeRGBA16Snorm`, `MTLDataTypeRGBA16Unorm`, `MTLDataTypeRGBA8Snorm`, `MTLDataTypeRGBA8Unorm`, `MTLDataTypeRGBA8Unorm_sRGB`
- **`MTLPixelFormat`**, 35 of 301: every MTLTextureDescriptor bearing it fails `-[MTLTextureDescriptorInternal validateWithDevice:]` with `has invalid pixelFormat` and the process ends on SIGABRT: measured over 350 shapes on an M3 Max / macOS 27.0.1 (all eight non-multisample texture types, both storage modes, newTextureWithDescriptor: and heapTextureSizeAndAlignWithDescriptor:), every one fatal and not one answering nil.
  Held by `cases/format_footprints.rs`: `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Uint`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Uint_PACKED`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Unorm`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Unorm_HLG`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Unorm_PACKED`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Unorm_PACKED_HLG`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Unorm_PACKED_PQ`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Unorm_PACKED_sRGB`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Unorm_PQ`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10Unorm_sRGB`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10_XR_PACKED`, `MTLPixelFormatBGRA10_XR_PACKED_sRGB`, `MTLPixelFormatR10Uint_PACKED`, `MTLPixelFormatR10Uint_X6`, `MTLPixelFormatR12Uint_PACKED`, `MTLPixelFormatR12Uint_X4`, `MTLPixelFormatRG10Uint_PACKED`, `MTLPixelFormatRG10Uint_X12`, `MTLPixelFormatRG12Uint_PACKED`, `MTLPixelFormatRG12Uint_X8`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB10A8Uint_2P`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB16Bfloat`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB16Float`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB16Sint`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB16Snorm`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB16Uint`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB16Unorm`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB32Float`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB32Sint`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB32Uint`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB8Sint`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB8Snorm`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB8Uint`, `MTLPixelFormatRGB8Unorm`, `MTLPixelFormatX24_Stencil8`

### By crossing, which is depth in the combinations

Every column above is per value, and the pairs are where most of the behaviour an implementation can get wrong lives: a side can be right about each value of two types alone and wrong about a cell of the two together. This counts the crossings, which is a third thing again — not reach, not per-value depth, but how many **pairs of the option space** a case puts to Metal at once.

A crossing is **declared**, in `scripts/conformance-coverage/crossings.tsv`: one row a crossing, naming the case module, the cell its falsification is read at (one of the module's recorded cases, or `family` where the cross is read across the family's cases rather than inside one of them), the Metal types crossed, and what only the cross decides. The count is therefore of crossings **declared**, never of crossings proved, and what makes the list worth reading is what the derivation refuses. **This document is not written** if a row names a module no case of which ran Metal, a cell that is not one of that module's recorded cases, an axis with no generated value list, fewer than two axes or one axis twice, or an axis of which the module's own text states **fewer than two values by name** — a module that cannot vary an axis cannot cross it, so one value would falsify the row.

What this axis cannot see, stated rather than implied:

- **Whether the expected answer needs both axes.** No reading of the text can say that: it is a property of the case's reasoning. The last column says it in words, for a reader to check against the case, and the refusals above check only that the claim is **stateable** — that the module varies both axes and the cell ran Metal.
- **A crossing nobody declared.** Every number here is a floor by exactly that. A family that crosses two types and carries no row counts for nothing, so this axis grows by declaration and not by discovery.
- **An axis that is not a Metal enum or option set.** A crossing of a type with a boolean or numeric Metal field — `alphaToCoverageEnabled`, a LOD clamp, an extent, a reset range — and a type crossed with **itself in two slots**, which the two stencil faces are, are real depth and are no pair of this option space. They are **not** uncounted: they are declared in `other_crossings.tsv` and counted in their own table below, apart from these numbers and never added into them.
- **An axis that is no Metal item at all.** A pixel format's class, the shape of a shader's output, whether a fragment writes its own sample mask: neither file can name one, because neither has anything to check such an axis against. That depth is in the suite and in no count here.
- **A module that takes its value list from the suite's shared vocabulary** states none of those values itself, so its cross cannot be declared until the module names them. The per-value columns above hold such values in their own *shared only* column for the same reason.
- **Which cell varies which axis.** The values are read from the **module's** text while the claim is about a cell of it, so a module whose cases each fix one axis reads as varying both. `family` says outright that the cross is read across the cases; a named cell is the one case the row's reasoning is read at.
- **A triple told apart from three pairs.** A row of three axes counts as its three pairs, and an interaction no pair of its axes can see is not distinguished from them.

|---|---|
| pairs of the option space declared | 24 |
| distinct pairs of Metal types crossed | 23 |
| types in at least one such pair | 25 |
| types with two or more values compared and in no crossing of **either** table | 20 |

| case module | cell | crossed | what only the cross decides |
|---|---|---|---|
| `blend_interactions` | `blend-interaction-mask-blend` | `MTLColorWriteMask` x `MTLBlendFactor` | whether a mask that drops alpha changes what an RGB factor reads from the destination's alpha. Neither sweep can ask it: the mask sweep's blend reads no channel but its own, and the factor sweep's mask keeps every channel |
| `blend_interactions` | `blend-interaction-mask-blend` | `MTLColorWriteMask` x `MTLBlendOperation` | whether the write mask is applied before the blend reads the destination or after it. Only `Min` and `Subtract` can see the order: under `Add`, `Max` and `ReverseSubtract` a channel the mask drops stores the destination either way, which is the one blend the mask's own sweep was written with |
| `blend_interactions` | its cases | `MTLBlendFactor` x `MTLPixelFormat` | whether Metal clamps the blend colour to `[0, 1]` before a factor multiplies by it. The float attachment alone reads as the colour not being clamped and the normalized one alone as the result being clamped like any overflow; the pair says the factor was 1 and not 2 |
| `border_swizzles` | `border-swizzles-rgba8unorm` | `MTLSamplerBorderColor` x `MTLTextureSwizzle` | whether a border texel goes through the texture's swizzle like any other texel: a side that swizzled the texel it fetched and not the border it substituted answers the colour's own channels in a swizzled lane |
| `border_swizzles` | its cases | `MTLPixelFormat` x `MTLSamplerBorderColor` | which channel of the border colour a format's channel reads. `A8Unorm`'s one channel takes the colour's **red**, so its opaque-black border reads alpha 0 where a four-channel format's reads 1 |
| `depth_resolve_pairs` | `resolve-depth-filters-4` | `MTLMultisampleDepthResolveFilter` x `MTLStoreAction` | whether the resolve is the same under both resolving store actions. Twenty-one cells say so under each, so a side that resolves from the stored copy under one and from the tile under the other diverges in a cell while each value alone is right |
| `depth_stencil_space` | `depthstencil-depth-slot` | `MTLCompareFunction` x `MTLStencilOperation` | which of the three stencil slots a fragment reaches, which the depth comparison and the stencil comparison decide together, and what the slot then writes: a side that routed a failed depth test to the stencil-failure slot is right about every comparison and every operation alone |
| `depth_stencil_space` | `depthstencil-facing-faces` | `MTLCullMode` x `MTLWinding` | whether the face whose rules a primitive takes is read from the same two settings as whether it is culled: a side that culls correctly and then applies the other face's operations leaves the other face's value in the stencil |
| `fetch_ordered` | its cases | `MTLColorWriteMask` x `MTLPixelFormat` | which memory bytes a channel mask keeps, which the format's channel order decides: a side that wrote the attachment through a channel-swapped view without swapping the mask is exact on one format and divergent on the other |
| `format_footprints` | its cases | `MTLPixelFormat` x `MTLTextureType` | which texture types Metal describes a texture of, which is the format's to decide: the one-plane `YCBCR*` and `RGB10A8_2P` formats answer a size and **no texture** for `Cube` and `CubeArray` and a texture for the other six types, while the padded and plain formats answer a texture for all eight |
| `heap_metal_sizes` | `heap-sizes-block-writes-only` | `MTLTextureUsage` x `MTLPixelFormat` x `MTLTextureType` | whether Apple's private `BlockWritesOnly` usage bit changes a heap answer. Every shape is recorded with the bit and without it, so a side whose layout depends on the bit for one format or one texture type and not another diverges in a cell while the bit alone and each format alone are right |
| `indirect_command_depth` | `indirect-render-commands-patch-draws` | `MTLIndirectCommandType` x `MTLTessellationControlPointIndexType` | whether an indirect patch draw reads its control-point indices from the command or from the pipeline that command holds. The buffer's descriptor declares both patch-draw types and each command carries its own pipeline, so the indexed type is the pipeline's and the index buffer is the command's argument: a side that encoded one command's type against the other's pipeline marks the grid the other owns, while the command types alone are a sweep and the index type alone is a direct draw's question |
| `int_attributes` | `integer-vertex-attributes` | `MTLVertexFormat` x `MTLVertexStepFunction` | whether the conversion of an integer attribute is the same whichever step function fetched the record. Metal's constant step is a fetch no fixed-function vertex input can express, so a side whose shader-pulled path converts unlike its fixed-function one is right about every format and every step function alone |
| `pass_sequence_actions` | `pass-sequence-color-actions` | `MTLStoreAction` x `MTLLoadAction` | whether the next pass over one attachment sees what this one stored. `MTLStoreActionStore` then `MTLLoadActionLoad` keeps pass A's draw and the host's own texels outside it; `MTLStoreActionDontCare` then `MTLLoadActionLoad` keeps **neither** of them, the texels the host wrote before pass A everywhere, so A's clear and A's draw are both gone; and under pass B's `MTLLoadActionClear` over the whole attachment the store action moves no texel at all. So neither value alone says anything: the 36 (load, store, options) triples of `pass_descriptors` only read the fields back, and every other pass case fixes one side of the boundary |
| `pixel_samplers` | its cases | `MTLPixelFormat` x `MTLSamplerMinMagFilter` x `MTLSamplerAddressMode` | what a pixel-coordinate sample reads at and past the texture's edge: the address mode decides which texel a tap outside falls on, the filter decides how many taps there are, and the format decides what their average rounds to. The family's sixteen cases are the cells |
| `primitive_type_crossings` | `primitive-type-restart-indices` | `MTLPrimitiveType` x `MTLIndexType` | whether the index value `0xffff` ends a primitive or names a vertex. The value that ends one is the index type's own maximum, and only a strip has a primitive for it to end: over one index run the two strips answer different covered sets under the two index types and the point, the line list and the triangle list answer the same set under both. The primitive type's own sweep states one index type a case over ordinary indices, and the index type's sweep never reaches a strip at its maximum, so neither can ask it |
| `render_state` | `render-state-facing` | `MTLCullMode` x `MTLWinding` | which of a triangle and its reverse survives a draw: flipping the winding flips it and flipping the cull mode flips it back, so neither setting's own value decides anything and a side that reads one and not the other draws the wrong one of the pair |
| `sampler_crossings` | its cases | `MTLSamplerBorderColor` x `MTLSamplerAddressMode` | whether a border-reading mode on one axis substitutes the border for a tap another axis put out of range. One case a border colour, and six address triples that put the border-reading mode on `s` alone, on `t` alone, on `r` alone and on all three: the sweeps that compared every colour read the border on `r` with `s` and `t` inside the texture, so no row of them asks which axis's mode the substitution follows |
| `sampler_crossings` | its cases | `MTLSamplerMinMagFilter` x `MTLSamplerMipFilter` | which level a sampler reads when the LOD clamp raises the requested one, and with which of the two filters -- `NotMipmapped` ignores the clamp outright, and the two mip filters pick their level and then their filter. The filter sweep samples at texel centres where a linear footprint is one texel, and the mip sweep pins both filters to nearest and says so, so neither can tell the three rules apart |
| `sampler_crossings` | `sampler-compare-filter-pairs` | `MTLCompareFunction` x `MTLSamplerMinMagFilter` | whether Metal filters the comparison's **results** or compares a filtered depth. Sixteen cells of every comparison against a nearest and a linear filter, sampled where four texels carry the footprint at weights of a quarter each and at 9/16, 3/16, 3/16 and 1/16: the comparison sweep samples at texel centres, where a linear footprint is one texel and all eight comparisons read the same under either rule |
| `shader_stencil` | `stencil-output` | `MTLCompareFunction` x `MTLStencilOperation` | which operation a fragment's `[[stencil]]` output fires, under a state whose two faces carry different comparisons and different operations: a side that fired the pass operation where the comparison failed writes a byte the comparison alone and the operation alone both allow |
| `stencil_resolved_sample` | `resolve-stencil-depth-resolved-sample` | `MTLMultisampleStencilResolveFilter` x `MTLMultisampleDepthResolveFilter` | which sample's stencil a combined attachment's resolve texture holds. Under `Sample0` it is sample 0's whatever the depth filter, and under `DepthResolvedSample` it is the stencil of the sample the depth filter took: `sample-0-between` answers 0x33 under all three depth filters on one stencil filter and 0x33, 0x11 and 0x55 on the other. A side that read the stencil filter and ignored the depth filter, or took the depth-picked sample whatever the stencil filter, is right on four of the six cells and wrong on two, and neither value's own sweep reaches them |
| `texture_descriptor_pairs` | `texture-pairs-usage-crossings` | `MTLTextureUsage` x `MTLPixelFormat` | which crossings of a usage with a format Metal's texture descriptor takes, and what it makes of them. `ShaderWrite` on `BC1_RGBA`, on `ASTC_4x4_LDR` and on `Depth32Float` each answer a texture of usage 0x3 and `RenderTarget` on the two block-compressed formats one of 0x4, where `ShaderAtomic` is fatal on every format but `R32Uint`: so the descriptor is where a guess puts the refusal and the pair is what says Metal does not put it there |
| `texture_descriptor_pairs` | `texture-pairs-usage-crossings` | `MTLTextureUsage` x `MTLTextureType` | whether a usage bit that bars lossless compression bars it in every texture type. A 1D texture of 5000 texels answers 32768 bytes under `ShaderRead` and under `ShaderRead | PixelFormatView` alike, because its height is 1 and no usage of it is ever compressed, where the same two usages part by 128 bytes on a 64 by 64 2D texture -- so the usage sweep in one type reads as the bit always moving the footprint |

#### Crossings that are not pairs of the option space

The table above counts pairs of two Metal **enums**, because that is what the per-value columns measure the depth of. A crossing of an enum with a Metal field that is a **boolean** or a **number**, and a crossing of one type with **itself in two or more slots**, is depth of the same kind and is no pair of that space. These are declared in `scripts/conformance-coverage/other_crossings.tsv`, in rows of the same four fields, and counted here. **The two counts are never added together**: one says how much of the option space's own grid a case puts to Metal, the other how much interaction the suite holds that the option space cannot express, and a sum of them would say neither.

An axis there is a Metal enum with a generated value list, which **may** be named more than once — that repetition is the type-in-two-slots crossing and the count of names is the count of slots — or one Metal item of one Metal type, spelled as the items axis keys it (`MTLRenderPipelineDescriptor.alphaToCoverageEnabled`, `MTLArgumentEncoder.setArgumentBuffer:offset:`).

**This document is not written** if such a row names a module no case of which ran Metal, a cell that is not one of that module's recorded cases, fewer than two axes, nothing that the cross decides, or an **enum** axis of which the module's own text states fewer than two values by name. Three refusals are this table's own:

- **a row every axis of which is a Metal enum named once.** That is a pair of the option space: it belongs in the table above, under the check that the module states two values of each axis, and a row parked here would be the same claim under a weaker rule;
- **a field axis `objc2-metal` binds no such item for, or one this module's own data never reaches.** The leg must hold a call of that field at a site whose guards this module's text satisfies. Reachability is the items axis's own join, so a field axis names a call this document already reports as exercised rather than a word a row's author chose; the guards are what make the row a claim about that **field** and not about any field of its Metal type. One shared encoder sets a pass descriptor's fields in one function, each under `if let Some(x) = pass.field`, so by reachability alone every case that opens a pass reaches them all — which would let a case attaching no visibility buffer declare a crossing on `visibilityResultBuffer`;
- **a field axis whose call answers a Metal type with a generated value list.** Such a field is an enum under another name — `MTLTextureDescriptor.storageMode` is one — and naming it as a field would dodge the two-values check the enum spelling carries.

What **cannot** be checked for a field axis, and is therefore what only a reader can check: that the module put **two values** of the field to Metal. A boolean's two values and a number's range are nowhere in the text this reads and in no recording's transcript, so a field axis carries the field being Metal's and this module stating it, and nothing about variation — and a field a module states as `None` or `false` reads here exactly like one it states a value for. For the same reason a **bare boolean** guard in the leg (`if descriptor.read_only {`) is no guard here: its body is reached on one of the field's two values, and which one a case states is what this cannot see. An enum axis carries the two-values check here exactly as it does above, which is why a row's enum axes are the stronger half of it. The last column, as above, is for a reader against the case.

| | |
|---|---|
| crossings declared that are not pairs of the option space | 30 |
| of those, naming a type in two or more slots | 8 |
| of those, naming a Metal field that is a boolean or a number | 23 |
| distinct Metal fields crossed | 27 |
| distinct Metal types crossed with themselves | 6 |

The two *of those* rows are not a partition and do not sum to the first: a row may name both, as the sampler row crossing its two filter slots with a LOD clamp does.

| case module | cell | crossed | what only the cross decides |
|---|---|---|---|
| `alpha_coverage_pairs` | `a2c-on-4-alpha-50-mask-no-alpha` | `MTLRenderPipelineDescriptor.alphaToCoverageEnabled` x `MTLColorWriteMask` | whether the alpha the coverage is read from is the fragment's own or what survives the attachment's write mask. A mask that drops alpha leaves the coverage exactly as the same alpha gives with the mask absent and moves only the stored alpha: the mask's own sweep reads a stored channel and never a coverage, and the alpha sweep never meets a mask |
| `alpha_coverage_pairs` | `a2one-on-1-blend-source-alpha` | `MTLRenderPipelineDescriptor.alphaToOneEnabled` x `MTLBlendFactor` | whether alpha-to-one replaces the alpha before or after the blend reads it. Under RGB factors of `SourceAlpha` and `OneMinusSourceAlpha` the field on stores the source alone and off stores it blended at 0.5, so the blend read 1 and not 0.5 -- where the field alone moves only the stored alpha and the blend alone gives the 0.5 answer at any setting |
| `alpha_coverage_pairs` | its cases | `MTLRenderPipelineDescriptor.alphaToCoverageEnabled` x `MTLRenderPipelineDescriptor.rasterSampleCount` | whether the coverage an alpha gives is `floor(alpha * rasterSampleCount)` or a round to nearest. At four samples an alpha of 0.5 covers two under either rule; the one-sample row is where they part, and it covers **none**, so the destination survives untouched where rounding would have stored the fragment. Neither sample count alone says which rule it is |
| `argument_encoder_crossings` | `argument-encoder-cross-offsets` | `MTLArgumentEncoder.setArgumentBuffer:offset:` x `MTLArgumentEncoder.setArgumentBuffer:startOffset:arrayElement:` | whether the two offsets compose or the second replaces the first. Over the 48-byte layout `(0, element 1)` writes word 6 and `(8, no element)` word 1, and `(8, element 1)` writes word 7 -- the cell neither call alone reaches. `constantDataAtIndex:` answers the constant's offset inside the arguments and so the same number at every cell, which is why the written mask is the site that reads it |
| `argument_encoder_crossings` | `argument-encoder-cross-type-mixtures` | `MTLArgumentDescriptor.constantBlockAlignment` x `MTLDataType` | whether `constantBlockAlignment` aligns the constant's offset inside the arguments or the encoder's own alignment. A single `MTLDataTypeUInt` at a block alignment of 64 is 4 bytes aligned to 4 -- the field changes nothing, because offset 0 is aligned to anything -- and a texture in front of it moves that constant to offset 64 and the encoder to 72 bytes while its alignment stays 8. So the field is indistinguishable alone and load-bearing one argument later |
| `argument_encoder_crossings` | `argument-encoder-cross-type-sizes` | `MTLDataType` x `MTLDataType` | whether a member's offset is a running sum of the members before it or a packing Metal may order. `char, char, float4, float4` is **48** bytes and `float4, char, float4, char` -- the same multiset -- is **64**, so a sweep of one type at a time sees two encoder lengths and only the permutation sees the layout |
| `indirect_command_depth` | `indirect-compute-commands-reset-and-ranges` | `MTLIndirectCommandBuffer.resetWithRange:` x `MTLComputeCommandEncoder.executeCommandsInBuffer:withRange:` | which commands of a buffer wrote anything, which the two ranges decide together: a command is blank where the reset covers it and runs where the execution does. A side that reset the executed range, or executed the reset one, is right about each range's own bounds and writes another command's segment |
| `indirect_command_depth` | `indirect-render-commands-inherited-state` | `MTLIndirectCommandBufferDescriptor.inheritBuffers` x `MTLIndirectRenderCommand.setVertexBuffer:offset:atIndex:` | which binding an executed command draws from when the parent encoder and the command have each bound one. With the field off the command's own binding is the only one there is, and with the command setting none the parent's is: each alone names a segment of the output, and only the pair says which of the two wins where both exist |
| `indirect_command_depth` | `indirect-render-commands-under-the-pass-state` | `MTLRenderCommandEncoder.setScissorRect:` x `MTLIndirectRenderCommand.drawPrimitives:vertexStart:vertexCount:instanceCount:baseInstance:` | which pixels an indirect draw covers, which the parent encoder's scissor and the command's own vertex range decide together -- a render command may not set a scissor of its own, so a side that dropped the pass's state at the execution rasterizes each half of the draw in full while being right about the scissor alone and about the draw alone |
| `layered_targets` | `layered-attachment-levels` | `MTLRenderPassAttachmentDescriptor.level` x `MTLRenderCommandEncoder.setScissorRect:` | whether a scissor rectangle is read in the **level's** own extent or in the target's level 0. A 2 by 2 rectangle at level 1 of an 8 by 8 target covers a quarter of the 4 by 4 level and not a sixteenth of the target, so the drawn region's own boundary says which extent the frame was. The level alone moves which plane is written and the rectangle alone moves a region of level 0, and neither says what the level's frame is |
| `layered_targets` | `layered-attachment-places` | `MTLRenderPassAttachmentDescriptor.slice` x `MTLRenderPassAttachmentDescriptor.depthPlane` | which of the two fields selects the plane rendered, which is the target's own shape's to decide: an array texture's plane is `slice`'s and a 3D texture's is `depthPlane`'s, so the same number in the other field reaches **nothing** and the plane renders at 0. Each field alone reads as the one that selects a plane, because the cell where it is 0 is the cell the other field was selecting with; only the pair says which field the shape listens to |
| `layered_targets` | `layered-depth-other-slice` | `MTLRenderPassAttachmentDescriptor.slice` x `MTLRenderPassAttachmentDescriptor.slice` | whether the slice a pass renders is the **attachment's** or the pass's. Each attachment takes its own: a pass whose colour is at slice 2 and whose depth is at slice 0 writes both, each at its own slice. The cell where the two agree is right under either rule, and only the two unequal cells part a side that took one attachment's slice for the whole pass -- which then leaves the other attachment's plane untouched and writes a plane nothing asked for |
| `layered_targets` | `layered-render-target-array-length` | `MTLRenderPassDescriptor.renderTargetArrayLength` x `MTLRenderPassAttachmentDescriptor.slice` | which slices a pass reaches: the layer count says how many and the attachment's slice says where they start, so the shader's layer `i` lands in slice `slice + i` and the load and store actions cover exactly those. At a count of 2 from slice 1 the pass writes slices 1 and 2 and leaves slice 0 and slice 3 as an earlier pass left them -- the cell a count at slice 0, or a slice at the default count, reads identically to a side that took the layer absolutely |
| `pass_attachment_pairs` | `pass-two-attachments-loads` | `MTLLoadAction` x `MTLLoadAction` | which attachment each load action is read from, in one pass whose two colour attachments state different ones. An earlier pass of the same command buffer stores a different value into each, so a `Load` shows a value no clear of this pass states and the two attachments' prior values differ: the two off-diagonal pairs are the cells only the pair reaches, and a side that applied colour 0's load action to colour 1 answers both diagonal cells exactly and both off-diagonal ones wrongly. The 36 (load, store, options) triples of `pass_descriptors` read the fields back off one attachment and never render two that disagree |
| `pass_attachment_pairs` | `pass-two-attachments-loads` | `MTLPixelFormat` x `MTLPixelFormat` | which attachment each format is read from. `RGBA32Float` beside `R32Float` and the swap make the two planes different sizes **and** leave attachment 1 holding red alone, so a side that took one attachment's format for the other cannot answer the cell at all -- where the equal pair answers the same under every rule, and a sweep of one format at a time is a one-attachment question that no pairing can falsify |
| `pass_attachment_pairs` | `pass-two-attachments-resolves` | `MTLStoreAction` x `MTLStoreAction` | which attachment's resolve texture a resolving store writes into: `MultisampleResolve` at one attachment beside `DontCare` at the other, which a depth-only pass has one attachment to state. Colour 0 resolving alone, colour 1 alone and both are the three cells, and a side that took attachment 0's resolve texture for attachment 1 writes colour 0's value into colour 1's texture in the two cells where attachment 1 resolves while answering the colour-0-alone cell exactly. An attachment stored `DontCare` carries no resolve texture, so neither slot's own sweep ever has a second attachment to confuse it with |
| `pass_bound_pairs` | `pass-bound-clear-reach` | `MTLRenderPassDescriptor.renderTargetWidth` x `MTLRenderPassDescriptor.renderTargetHeight` x `MTLLoadAction` x `MTLRenderCommandEncoder.drawPrimitives:vertexStart:vertexCount:instanceCount:baseInstance:` | which texels of an attachment **larger than its pass's render area** the pass changes, and to what. A `Clear` load clears from the origin over the render area rounded up to whole 32-pixel tiles and clipped to the attachment where the pass encodes a draw, the whole level and slice where it encodes none, and a `Load` load keeps every texel whatever the area: so each axis moves a plane only at the others' settings and a case that fixes two of the three says nothing about the other cells. The two numbers are separate axes because the rounding is **per axis** -- the 8 by 40 and 40 by 8 cells are where a side that rounded the larger side, or took one axis's reach for both, answers the square cells exactly and those two wrongly -- and `draw-covers-nothing` beside `no-draw-call` is where a side that read the draw axis as whether a draw *wrote* anything answers two cells alike and both wrongly. The number sweep of `pass_descriptors` reads the two numbers back off a descriptor and renders through neither |
| `pass_sequence_actions` | `pass-sequence-aspect-actions` | `MTLStoreAction` x `MTLStoreAction` x `MTLLoadAction` x `MTLLoadAction` | which attachment each aspect's action is read from, across a pass boundary where the two aspects disagree in both passes. Pass A stores the depth and discards the stencil, or the reverse, and pass B loads one aspect and clears the other: the depth plane is then a function of the depth's two slots and the stencil plane of the stencil's, and a side that took one aspect's slot for both answers the three symmetric cells exactly and the six asymmetric ones wrongly. Measured: taking the depth attachment's action for the stencil's scratch copy moves four of the nine cells, every one of them in the stencil plane and none in the depth plane |
| `pass_sequence_actions` | `pass-sequence-color-actions` | `MTLRenderPassDescriptor.renderTargetWidth` x `MTLStoreAction` x `MTLLoadAction` | whether the next pass's render area bounds what its load action does to what the last pass stored. A `MTLLoadActionLoad` under a 32 by 32 area of a 40 by 40 attachment keeps every texel, so the bound moves **not one** against the whole-area cell -- the independence `pass_bound_pairs` measured inside one pass, holding across the boundary. A `MTLLoadActionClear` under the same bound clears its own tiles and leaves the 8 texels past them holding **what pass A's store action left there**, so the store action is visible through a `Clear` after all, which it is not over the whole attachment. Neither the area alone nor either action alone reaches either cell |
| `sampler_crossings` | its cases | `MTLSamplerMinMagFilter` x `MTLSamplerMinMagFilter` x `MTLSamplerDescriptor.lodMinClamp` | which of the two filters reads the level `lodMinClamp` raised the requested one to. The two `(min, mag)` pairs whose filters agree answer the same under every rule, so only an unequal pair at a clamp above the sampled level parts Metal's rule from Vulkan's -- the magnifying filter at a level of zero or less, the minifying one above it -- and from a `NotMipmapped` sampler's, which ignores the clamp outright |
| `sampler_crossings` | `sampler-axis-border-opaque-black` | `MTLSamplerAddressMode` x `MTLSamplerAddressMode` x `MTLSamplerAddressMode` | whether a border colour is substituted per **axis** or for the sample. On a lane whose `s` is inside the texture and whose `t` is a period out, a sampler whose `s` mode is `ClampToBorderColor` and whose `t` mode repeats reads either the border or the wrapped texel, and no sampler whose border-reading axis is itself the out-of-range one can part the two readings |
| `sampler_crossings` | `sampler-axis-mode-triples` | `MTLSamplerAddressMode` x `MTLSamplerAddressMode` x `MTLSamplerAddressMode` | whether each axis's own mode decides that axis's wrap. Three **unequal** modes over a grid of one inside coordinate and four outside are what tell it from a decision taken on the coordinate as a whole, or from one axis's mode applied to all three: every sampler whose axes agree, and every sweep that moves one axis with the others repeating, reads the right texel under all three rules |
| `tessellation_crossings` | its cases | `MTLPatchType` x `MTLTessellationPartitionMode` x `MTLRenderCommandEncoder.setTessellationFactorBuffer:offset:instanceStride:` | how many of a patch's inside factors the mode's rule is applied to, which is the patch type's. Moving the second inside factor from 2 to 5 moves every quad cell under every mode and **no** triangle cell under any mode, while moving the first moves both: a quad's function reads two inside factors and a triangle's reads one. No case states two inside factors of a triangle, so the half of the claim that the second slot is ignored there is this cross's alone |
| `tessellation_crossings` | `tessellation-partition-degenerates` | `MTLTessellationPartitionMode` x `MTLRenderCommandEncoder.setTessellationFactorBuffer:offset:instanceStride:` | whether a factor that is not a positive number meets Metal's cull or a partition's clamp, and which. An edge factor of 0 marks nothing under all four modes, `fractionalEven`'s floor of 2 included, so the cull runs before any mode's range; a zero or NaN **inside** factor, which no cull reads, answers one set under each mode and `fractionalOdd`'s is not the other three's. The degenerate sweep states one mode, so it cannot say the clamped answer is the mode's, and the mode sweep states finite factors, so it never meets either rule |
| `tessellation_crossings` | `tessellation-partition-factors` | `MTLTessellationPartitionMode` x `MTLRenderCommandEncoder.setTessellationFactorBuffer:offset:instanceStride:` | which of the four modes answer the same point set, which is the **factor's** to decide: five of the six pairs of modes agree at one of the eight factors stated here and part at another, and two of those partings are the clamp range's own ends -- `fractionalOdd` stops one below the ceiling and `fractionalEven` starts at 2. The mode sweep states one factor list, so every pair of modes there either agrees or parts once and for all, and the factor sweep states one mode, so it reads a clamp and no rounding |
| `tessellation_crossings` | `tessellation-partition-scales` | `MTLTessellationPartitionMode` x `MTLRenderCommandEncoder.setTessellationFactorScale:` | what a mode's rule does with the fraction a scale produced. `setTessellationFactorScale:` is the one way to hand a partition rule a factor a `half` factor buffer could not have held: `integer` and `fractionalEven` answer the same cell set at both whole products stated here, 8 and 10, and different sets at both products that are not whole, 8.5 and 2.5. The scale sweep states one mode and the mode sweep sets no scale |
| `tessellation_sweeps` | `tessellation-factor-steps` | `MTLTessellationFactorStepFunction` x `MTLRenderCommandEncoder.drawPatches:patchStart:patchCount:patchIndexBuffer:patchIndexBufferOffset:instanceCount:baseInstance:` | which factor record a patch reads, which is the step function's rule applied to the draw's own `patchStart`, `instanceCount` and `baseInstance`. The case's four legs are chosen so that no two of the four step functions answer all four of them, which takes both axes: a per-patch step and a constant step part only at a patch start above 0, and a per-instance step parts from both only where the draw states more than one instance |
| `texture_descriptor_pairs` | `texture-pairs-plain-contents` | `MTLTextureDescriptor.allowGPUOptimizedContents` x `MTLTextureUsage` | whether lossless compression is a **conjunction** of the flag with the usage or either one's doing. The flag off answers the uncompressed footprint at a usage that would have compressed, and at `ShaderRead | PixelFormatView` it moves nothing because the usage already barred it: so neither the flag's own sweep nor the usage's says which of the two switches moved the number |
| `texture_descriptor_pairs` | `texture-pairs-type-levels-slices` | `MTLTextureType` x `MTLTextureDescriptor.mipmapLevelCount` x `MTLTextureDescriptor.arrayLength` | how many bytes a mip chain takes and how many levels Metal allows, both of which are the **type's** to decide. An 8 by 8 by 64 3D texture takes seven levels and its footprint saturates at four; a 2D array of one slice is 16512 bytes aligned to 16384 where the plain 2D of that extent is 16512 aligned to 128, and at seven levels they part to 33152 against 22528; a cube is six of the array's chain. A sweep over types at one level count, beside a sweep over level counts of one type, answers none of it |
| `texture_descriptor_pairs` | `texture-pairs-usage-crossings` | `MTLTextureUsage` x `MTLTextureDescriptor.height` | whether a usage bit that bars lossless compression bars it at every extent. 16 by 16 answers 1152 bytes against 1024 and 16 by 15 answers 1024 against 1024, so one texel of height decides whether the usage moves the footprint at all -- and a usage sweep at one extent reads as the bit always moving it |

#### Types with per-value depth and no crossing

The work list of this axis, and the shape of the answer to *is the depth sufficient?*: each of these is a type the suite compared two or more values of and crossed with nothing in **either** table. The number is the values compared. A row is a question and not a verdict — the type may be crossed by a family that declared no row, and a type whose values cannot interact with anything has nothing to answer — but a type deep on the per-value axis and absent here is where a cell of a pair is least likely to have been looked at.

- **`MTLAttributeFormat`**, 53 of 54 compared
- **`MTLBindingType`**, 8 of 12 compared
- **`MTLFunctionType`**, 6 of 7 compared
- **`MTLFunctionOptions`**, 5 of 5 compared
- **`MTLRenderStages`**, 5 of 5 compared
- **`MTLStepFunction`**, 5 of 9 compared
- **`MTLPrimitiveTopologyClass`**, 4 of 4 compared
- **`MTLBindingAccess`**, 3 of 3 compared
- **`MTLHazardTrackingMode`**, 3 of 3 compared
- **`MTLMutability`**, 3 of 3 compared
- **`MTLPipelineOption`**, 3 of 4 compared
- **`MTLSamplerReductionMode`**, 3 of 3 compared
- **`MTLSparsePageSize`**, 3 of 3 compared
- **`MTLVisibilityResultMode`**, 3 of 3 compared
- **`MTLBlitOption`**, 2 of 4 compared
- **`MTLDepthClipMode`**, 2 of 2 compared
- **`MTLHeapType`**, 2 of 3 compared
- **`MTLResourceUsage`**, 2 of 3 compared
- **`MTLStoreActionOptions`**, 2 of 2 compared
- **`MTLTriangleFillMode`**, 2 of 2 compared

### Types a case is about, whose values this axis cannot read

The raw-ordinal limit above as a work list rather than a sentence. Each row is a type whose name a recorded case module uses and **none of whose values that module states by name**: a sweep written `for bits in 0..8` reads exactly this way, and so does a module that only mentions the type in passing. So a row is a question for the case, never a verdict on it -- the module may put every value of the type to Metal, and this axis would still count none of them.

It names the type and the module, and no value: which value a computed number is is not a property of the text, and a rule that answered it would be inventing the answer. Closing a row means the case stating its values by name, or a transcript site recording the enumerator beside the ordinal it already stores.

| Metal type | values | compared | modules naming it that state no value |
|---|---|---|---|
| `MTLBarrierScope` | 3 | 1 | `compute_encoder_commands`, `mesh_barriers` |
| `MTLIndexType` | 2 | 2 | `tessellation_sweeps` |
| `MTLBindingAccess` | 3 | 3 | `texture_accesses` |
| `MTLLoadAction` | 3 | 3 | `srgb_fetch` |
| `MTLAttributeFormat` | 54 | 53 | `attribute_formats` |
| `MTLVertexFormat` | 54 | 54 | `vertex_formats`, `wide_int_attributes` |

### The values not compared

The work list. A name here is a value of Metal's that no committed recording holds and no case module states **by name**: it is not a claim that Metal accepts the value on every device, and not a claim that no case reaches it, because a case that sweeps a type by ordinal states none of its values here. Each row is a question for the case, not a verdict on it.

- **`MTLBindingType`**, 4 of 12: `MTLBindingTypePrimitiveAccelerationStructure`, `MTLBindingTypeInstanceAccelerationStructure`, `MTLBindingTypeIntersectionFunctionTable`, `MTLBindingTypeTensor`
- **`MTLStepFunction`**, 4 of 9: `MTLStepFunctionPerVertex`, `MTLStepFunctionPerInstance`, `MTLStepFunctionPerPatch`, `MTLStepFunctionPerPatchControlPoint`
- **`MTLReadWriteTextureTier`**, 3 of 3: `MTLReadWriteTextureTierNone`, `MTLReadWriteTextureTier1`, `MTLReadWriteTextureTier2`
- **`MTLArgumentBuffersTier`**, 2 of 2: `MTLArgumentBuffersTier1`, `MTLArgumentBuffersTier2`
- **`MTLBarrierScope`**, 2 of 3: `MTLBarrierScopeBuffers`, `MTLBarrierScopeRenderTargets`
- **`MTLDataType`**, 2 of 97: `MTLDataTypeNone`, `MTLDataTypeTensor`
- **`MTLAttributeFormat`**, 1 of 54: `MTLAttributeFormatInvalid`
- **`MTLFunctionType`**, 1 of 7: `MTLFunctionTypeIntersection`
- **`MTLResourceUsage`**, 1 of 3: `MTLResourceUsageSample`

And the values only the suite's shared sources name, which some case reaches and this cannot attribute:

- **`MTLBlitOption`**: `MTLBlitOptionNone`, `MTLBlitOptionRowLinearPVRTC`
- **`MTLCPUCacheMode`**: `MTLCPUCacheModeWriteCombined`
- **`MTLCommandBufferStatus`**: `MTLCommandBufferStatusNotEnqueued`, `MTLCommandBufferStatusEnqueued`, `MTLCommandBufferStatusCommitted`, `MTLCommandBufferStatusScheduled`, `MTLCommandBufferStatusCompleted`, `MTLCommandBufferStatusError`
- **`MTLDispatchType`**: `MTLDispatchTypeSerial`, `MTLDispatchTypeConcurrent`
- **`MTLHeapType`**: `MTLHeapTypeSparse`
- **`MTLIndirectCommandType`**: `MTLIndirectCommandTypeDrawMeshThreads`
- **`MTLPipelineOption`**: `MTLPipelineOptionArgumentInfo`
- **`MTLPurgeableState`**: `MTLPurgeableStateNonVolatile`, `MTLPurgeableStateVolatile`, `MTLPurgeableStateEmpty`
- **`MTLSparseTextureRegionAlignmentMode`**: `MTLSparseTextureRegionAlignmentModeOutward`, `MTLSparseTextureRegionAlignmentModeInward`
- **`MTLStorageMode`**: `MTLStorageModeShared`, `MTLStorageModeManaged`, `MTLStorageModePrivate`, `MTLStorageModeMemoryless`

### Metal types with no generated value list

These the tree models by hand: a Rust item whose own doc comment opens by naming a Metal type, and whose shape is a closed enum or a newtype over one integer. The tree declares them itself because the compile-and-plan layer never reads them, and that stays — but a declaration measured against itself is no measurement, so `scripts/regen-metal-enums/`'s `TABLE_ONLY` list generates `scripts/conformance-coverage/metal_enumerators.tsv`, the SDK's own values for them, and every type it covers is in the per-type table above with a real denominator. What is left here is what the table does not cover.

For a row here **this axis has no denominator**: *declared* is our own sources' variant count rather than the SDK's — a newtype option set declares none — and there is no *answered* column, because a recording names a value by its Metal enumerator and this has no list of those names to match against.

1 are left, and the list is a floor: a type the tree models without saying so on the item lands in neither table, and so is nowhere in this axis at all.

| Metal type | the tree's type | declared | stated by a recorded case | shared only |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `MTLResourceOptions` | `ResourceOptions` | — a bit set | 0 | 0 |

## Exercised, but not ours

An item the suite's Metal leg calls whose coverage-map status is not implemented. Read each one before concluding anything: a call the leg makes to **set its own Metal up** is not a behaviour under test, and our side need not have it. A call the case's answer depends on is the other thing, and would be a case that can only be exact by accident.

## Called, attributed to no family

An item the Metal leg calls that no case module was found to reach: a `Side` method no case calls, a step of the vocabulary no case encodes, or a call the leg makes outside every `Side` method — the side's own constructor, which runs for every case and which this walk has no family to attribute, because a family is read off which `Side` methods a case's text calls. The first two are suite code that no case runs; the third is measured and named here rather than counted as exercised.

| item | status |
|---|---|
| the header's free functions `MTLCreateSystemDefaultDevice` | implemented |
| `MTLDevice` `name` | implemented |
| `MTLIndirectRenderCommand` `setObjectThreadgroupMemoryLength:atIndex:` | implemented |

## Called, not in the coverage map

The Metal leg sends these selectors to a receiver this typed, and `scripts/metal-coverage/coverage.tsv` holds no row for the item. Either the selector is `NSObject`'s, which `objc2-metal` generates on every class it binds and no Metal header declares, or the map is short of the SDK, or the name is spelled differently there; the last two are worth a look.
